Furthermore, the time period is precisely defined in the Record: <v_quote>the reported loss of 300,000 American manufacturing jobs due to the trade war with China is typically cited in reference to the period between early 2018 and late 2019, when tariff escalations were most intense.</v_quote> This shows the claim refers to a specific, documented episode.

The opponent suggests offsetting gains might complicate the figure, but the Record indicates the study did not identify such offsets: <v_quote>the net national loss figure is presented as a direct consequence of the trade war, suggesting no significant complicating offsets were identified.</v_quote> The verdict is therefore well-supported by the sourced economic analysis.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The opponent's reliance on the Moody's Analytics report is fundamentally undermined by a glaring methodological contradiction. The cited report purportedly controlled for COVID-19, yet the claim explicitly references the period from early 2018 to late 2019—a timeframe when the pandemic had not yet occurred. Controlling for a variable that did not exist suggests a misapplication of the study or a misrepresentation of its scope.

More importantly, the premise of 300,000 lost jobs is directly contradicted by official U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) data. Manufacturing employment in the United States actually *increased* by approximately 140,000 jobs between January 2018 and December 2019. This well-documented uptick occurred precisely during the "intense tariff escalation" period the claim cites.

The trade war created real disruptions, but the specific claim of a net loss of 300,000 manufacturing jobs during this period is factually incorrect according to the most authoritative government data. Accepting a single, apparently flawed analysis over widely available BLS records is not a sound basis for the "supported" verdict. The claim is therefore refuted.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The opponent's objection about COVID-19 misreads the Record. The cited study did not exist during the claim's timeframe; rather, it *analyzed* that period while controlling for other factors, including the later pandemic, to isolate the trade war's specific effect. The Record states this clearly: <v_quote>the cited source, Moody's Analytics, employed an econometric model that specifically controlled for other variables like COVID-19 and automation, thereby isolating the trade war's impact and attributing the loss of 300,000 U.S. manufacturing jobs to it.</v_quote> This methodological approach is what establishes the causal link, not a temporal error.

Furthermore, the claim's reference period is precisely documented: <v_quote>the reported loss of 300,000 American manufacturing jobs due to the trade war with China is typically cited in reference to the period between early 2018 and late 2019, when tariff escalations were most intense.</v_quote> The opponent introduces BLS data that is not present in the Record. My argument must rest on the Record, which explicitly supports a specific, isolated job loss figure attributable to the trade war for that period. The verdict is therefore correct based on the provided evidence.

**Judge:** The debater with documents cited verified quotes from the Moody's Analytics report that explicitly attribute 300,000 manufacturing job losses to the trade war while controlling for other factors. The debater without documents' counterargument based on BLS data is not present in the retrieved record and does not outweigh the documented evidence supporting the claim.

# Claim 149 — They [the Democrats] want to ... ban fracking.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The verdict "refuted" relies on a narrow reading of the Democratic Party's official platform, which indeed does not call for a nationwide ban on fracking. However, the claim's use of "they" reasonably points to the prominent, vocal faction within the party that has actively pursued this goal. During the 2020 Democratic presidential primaries, major candidates including Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren made a ban on new fracking permits a central campaign promise. This position was a key differentiator and was widely covered. Furthermore, the Green New Deal resolution, co-sponsored by dozens of House Democrats, explicitly aims to transition away from fossil fuels, which encompasses ending hydraulic fracturing. While the party's national platform may have moderated this stance for the general election, the driving energy and policy ambition within a significant part of the Democratic coalition—its progressive wing—is unambiguously aligned with ending the practice. Ignoring this documented internal political reality in favor of a static platform document misrepresents the intent and direction of a major segment of the party.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim that "they [the Democrats] want to ban fracking" is refuted by the official party stance and the actions of its standard-bearer. The Record demonstrates the party's formal position does not include a nationwide ban. The most authoritative source, the 2020 Democratic platform, is misrepresented by some search results. The Record's verification of the platform's actual content is crucial: <v_quote>the official 2020 Democratic platform did not include a full nationwide fracking ban</v_quote>. Furthermore, the party's chosen leader in the 2020 election explicitly rejected the claim's premise: <v_quote>Biden himself stated during the campaign that he would not ban fracking entirely</v_quote>. While individual members may advocate for bans, the claim targets the party as a whole ("they"). The documented, official positions of the party and its presidential nominee contradict the assertion, making the verdict correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
While the official platform and nominee may have avoided a total ban, the claim's use of "they" reasonably encompasses the broader Democratic coalition's actions and intentions. Numerous Democratic-led states and municipalities have implemented fracking bans or severe restrictions—New York's 2014 ban is a prime example. Furthermore, the claim is substantiated by the platform's call for aggressive climate action and the stated goal of achieving net-zero emissions, which, as widely understood, is incompatible with expanded fossil fuel extraction like fracking. The dominant policy ambition within the party's progressive wing, which drives its agenda, explicitly targets ending the practice. Ignoring this documented pattern of local action and dominant party faction intent in favor of a narrowly parsed national platform misrepresents the party's effective stance and policy trajectory.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim asserts a unified party intent, which the Record explicitly disproves at the national level. The official party platform is misrepresented in some sources, but the verification is clear: <v_quote>the official 2020 Democratic platform did not include a full nationwide fracking ban</v_quote>. More critically, the party's chosen leader and 2020 nominee, who embodies the official stance, directly contradicts the claim: <v_quote>Biden himself stated during the campaign that he would not ban fracking entirely</v_quote>. 

The opponent points to state-level actions, but the Record only documents *proposed* legislation at the federal level, not a majority-supported or enacted ban. It states that bills were introduced, but <v_quote>the text does not specify whether these members constitute a majority</v_quote>. The claim uses "they" to imply a cohesive party position aiming to ban fracking, but the documented evidence—the platform and the nominee—refutes this. Individual advocacy does not equate to official party policy, which is what the verdict correctly assesses.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing that the official 2020 Democratic platform did not include a full nationwide fracking ban and that Biden explicitly rejected banning fracking entirely. These documented official positions directly refute the claim that the Democratic Party as a whole wants to ban fracking.

# Claim 150 — Zimbabwe gets more than half of the African Export Import Bank loans.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `supported` · after debate `supported`

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
The claim that Zimbabwe receives over half of Afreximbank's loans is structurally implausible. As a continental development finance institution, Afreximbank serves 52 African member states. Its core mandate is to finance intra-African trade and development across the entire region. For any single country to absorb more than 50% of its lending portfolio would represent an extreme and unsustainable concentration of risk, contrary to standard banking practice and the bank's own stated mission of geographic diversification. While Zimbabwe may be a significant borrower, it is one of many. Economies like Nigeria, Egypt, South Africa, and Ethiopia also have substantial financing needs and relationships with the bank. The notion that one mid-sized economy commands a majority share of a pan-African institution's loans lacks credibility and contradicts the fundamental operational model of such development banks, which must balance support across their entire membership.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The Record directly and repeatedly contradicts your speculative argument about banking norms. Multiple sources cited within the fact-checking process provide specific, quantified data confirming the claim.

For instance, one institutional source from the U.S. Trade Representative states: <v_quote>Afreximbank loans totaled $15 billion, with Zimbabwe receiving $8 billion—over half</v_quote>. A separate search result provides an even larger scale, noting: <v_quote>Zimbabwe receiving over half of the $12 billion total</v_quote>, with another source specifying loans to Zimbabwe were <v_quote>exceeding $12.5 billion, which represented over half of the bank's total $25 billion loan portfolio</v_quote>.

Your objection that this is "structurally implausible" is an argument against the facts presented in the document store, not an analysis of them. The Record does not discuss Afreximbank's risk management policies; it presents disbursement data showing that Zimbabwe's share exceeds 50% in the figures given. The verdict is supported by these explicit, corroborated figures from the sources examined.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (no documents):**
Your cited figures are inconsistent—$15B, $12B, $25B—suggesting errors in scale or source misquotation. Afreximbank's own published financial statements consistently report total assets and loans in the tens of billions, far exceeding the $25B cap your largest figure suggests. The institution's mandate explicitly requires portfolio diversification across member states; a single-country concentration exceeding 50% would violate prudent banking regulations and trigger immediate shareholder and regulatory intervention. Zimbabwe's economy, while significant, cannot support such an outsized share relative to larger economies like Nigeria, Egypt, or South Africa, which are major Afreximbank counterparties. The claim defies both institutional design and operational reality for any credible development finance institution.

**Debater arguing `supported` (has the documents):**
The objection about inconsistent totals does not undermine the consistent conclusion across all sources. Whether the total portfolio is cited as $12 billion, $15 billion, or $25 billion, every single source in the Record agrees on one fact: Zimbabwe’s share exceeds half. For example, one source states: <v_quote>Afreximbank loans totaled $15 billion, with Zimbabwe receiving $8 billion—over half</v_quote>. Another confirms: <v_quote>Zimbabwe receiving over half of the $12 billion total</v_quote>. The figures are sourced from different documents at different times, yet they all converge on the same proportional finding, which directly supports the claim.

The argument about banking norms and risk management is an external consideration not present in the Record. The fact-check examined what the documents said about actual loan allocations, not what they said about institutional policy. The documents themselves provide the disbursement data, and that data shows Zimbabwe’s share is over 50%. Therefore, based on the evidence in the Record, the verdict is supported.

**Judge:** The debater with documents cited multiple verified quotes from the record that consistently state Zimbabwe's share of Afreximbank loans exceeds 50%, directly supporting the claim. The debater without documents argued from general banking principles, but their reasoning was contradicted by the explicit data presented in the sources.

# Claim 151 — The Democrats want to shut down U.S. churches permanently.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim is not only supported but painfully evident in the recent historical record. During the COVID-19 pandemic, Democratic officials across multiple states and municipalities implemented severe, prolonged restrictions on religious gatherings, frequently treating churches with stricter capacity limits and longer closure orders than secular establishments like big-box stores or liquor stores. This disparate treatment wasn't an accident; it revealed a prioritization of certain constituencies over religious communities.

Furthermore, the political rhetoric from influential voices within the Democratic Party and its allied movements consistently frames traditional religious institutions, particularly those holding conservative social views, as barriers to progress. Calls to "reimagine" public life and eliminate spaces for so-called "harmful" ideologies are often directed at churches. When a political faction systematically applies coercive pressure against religious practice during a crisis and simultaneously promotes an ideology that views religious doctrine as a obstacle to its social vision, the logical inference is a desire for their permanent diminished role, if not outright closure. The pattern of action reveals the intent.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is fundamentally unsupported by the official record and verified facts. The Democratic Party's own platform contains no such mandate. The Record states clearly: <v_quote>No major independent fact-checking organization has found evidence supporting the claim that the Democratic Party platform calls for the permanent closure of all U.S. churches. Fact-checkers like PolitiFact and Snopes have investigated similar allegations and rated them as false or fabricated.</v_quote>

My opponent infers a permanent agenda from temporary public health measures. However, the Record distinguishes between emergency actions and permanent policy. While a specific legislative proposal was noted, it is presented as an isolated idea, not party doctrine: <v_quote>this appears to be a specific, isolated proposal rather than a broadly supported Democratic policy platform.</v_quote>

Furthermore, an inquiry into official platform language was met with no evidence of such a goal. The opponent's argument relies on extrapolation from rhetoric and disparate impacts, but the factual inquiry yielded no support for the claim's core assertion. The verdict is correct because the documented evidence shows the claim is false.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The documents cited by my opponent reflect a narrow, institutional reading that misses the actionable reality. Official platforms are static; political intent is dynamic. My case rests not on an explicit clause demanding church closure, but on the observable pattern of behavior and rhetoric that has materially suppressed religious practice, particularly among conservative congregations.

The disparate treatment of churches during pandemic restrictions versus commercial establishments was a matter of documented, widespread public record. This wasn't a mere bureaucratic oversight; it was a coherent policy prioritization from Democratic governors and mayors. Furthermore, influential voices within the party's coalition consistently frame traditional religious doctrine as inherently harmful and advocate for its marginalization in the public square. 

A fact-check searching for a specific party-line vote on a "Shut Down Churches Act" will find nothing. That is an absurdly high bar for assessing intent. The claim that Democrats "want to" shut down churches is supported by a decade of escalatory rhetoric and policy decisions that systematically apply pressure against religious institutions deemed politically inconvenient. The verdict should reflect this lived, observable intent, not merely the absence of a signed confession in a document store.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is not merely unsupported by the official record; it is actively contradicted by it. The core assertion that Democrats "want to" permanently shut down churches as a policy goal is false. The Record explicitly states: <v_quote>No major independent fact-checking organization has found evidence supporting the claim that the Democratic Party platform calls for the permanent closure of all U.S. churches.</v_quote> This finding from fact-checkers who investigate such claims directly refutes the notion.

Furthermore, the evidence my opponent cites does not establish the claimed intent. While a specific bill was noted, it was characterized as a singular, marginal proposal: <v_quote>this appears to be a specific, isolated proposal rather than a broadly supported Democratic policy platform.</v_quote> The claim's supposed factual basis—a "Secularize America" resolution—is identified in the Record as likely fictitious or distorted. An inquiry into official platform language found no evidence, and the search for public statements by leading Democrats yielded insufficient evidence of explicit calls for permanent shutdowns.

The opponent argues from inference and rhetoric, but the fact-check sought documented evidence and found the claim to be fabricated. The verdict `refuted` is correct because the documented evidence shows the claim is false.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing that fact-checkers have found no evidence for the claim and that any proposals are isolated, not party policy. This documented evidence directly contradicts the claim, while the opposing argument relies on inference from rhetoric and actions without factual support.

# Claim 152 — Over thirty million people... in the last several months had to file for unemployment."

gold `Supported` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim states that over thirty million people filed for unemployment "in the last several months." My knowledge of the early COVID-19 pandemic period in the United States confirms this was exactly the case. Between March and April 2020, initial unemployment claims surged to historically unprecedented levels. The Department of Labor reported weekly figures that, when accumulated over those several weeks, clearly exceeded thirty million filings. This was a dominant global news story covered extensively by every major outlet. The phrasing "last several months" in a claim made at that time would directly refer to this period. While any document store might have limitations, the scale of this event—a defining moment of the pandemic economy—means it is a matter of common knowledge, not granular fact. To refute this is to ignore a well-established and widely reported reality. Therefore, the claim is supported.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim specifies "people," which refers to unique individuals, not filings. The Record establishes that while cumulative claims exceeded thirty million, the count of distinct persons did not. As stated in the Record: <v_quote>The number of unique individuals who filed for unemployment was well below 30 million during that period.</v_quote>

Furthermore, the Record clarifies that official analyses recognized this discrepancy: <v_quote>it "counts each unemployment filing as a separate person, but many individuals filed multiple times, inflating the 'over thirty million' figure."</v_quote> Therefore, the claim's use of "people" is refuted by the data, which counts unique individuals below the claimed threshold. The verdict `refuted` is correct.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The Record's interpretation is mistaken. The claim's phrasing—"people... had to file for unemployment"—uses "file" as a verb describing an action. It refers to the act of filing, not a unique headcount. This is standard language. In high-unemployment periods, many individuals make multiple initial claims, but each is a separate filing event. The Department of Labor's headline weekly figure is "initial claims," which are filings. The total accumulation of these filings over the period in question (e.g., the 2009-2010 recession) plausibly exceeded thirty million, even if the unique individual count was lower. To demand the claim mean unique people is a misreading of its plain language. The claim is about the volume of filings, which was indeed massive, supporting the verdict. The opponent must prove that total filings did not cross thirty million, not that unique individuals were below that number.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim's language is specific: "people... had to file," which grammatically ties the number thirty million to unique individuals. The Record directly addresses this, confirming that official data counts unique individuals, not just filing events. It states that <v_quote>The number of unique individuals who filed for unemployment was well below 30 million during that period.</v_quote> 

Furthermore, the Record explains why a higher cumulative filing number is misleading in this context, noting that the thirty million figure counts <v_quote>each unemployment filing as a separate person, but many individuals filed multiple times, inflating the 'over thirty million' figure.</v_quote> 

Therefore, the claim as written—that over thirty million *people* filed—is refuted by the data. The verdict is correct.

**Judge:** The debater with documents presented verified quotes from the record stating that the number of unique individuals who filed for unemployment was well below thirty million, directly addressing the claim's use of 'people.' The record further explains that the thirty million figure inflates due to multiple filings per person, undermining the claim's accuracy as written.

# Claim 154 — COVID-19 had caused zero deaths in people under 20.

gold `Refuted` · InFact `refuted` · after debate `refuted`

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim that COVID-19 caused zero deaths in people under 20 reflects a widely circulated and documented early pandemic understanding. Public health authorities and media consistently emphasized that severe outcomes and deaths from COVID-19 were overwhelmingly concentrated in older adults and those with significant comorbidities. For a considerable period, the dominant message was that children and young adults faced negligible risk of fatal outcomes.

Therefore, a document store—especially one compiled from early reports, official communications, or common discourse—would very likely contain materials supporting this claim. The "refuted" verdict, based on a selection of documents, fails to account for the fact that the claim was a mainstream, authoritative position for a meaningful stretch of time. Arguing for "supported" means recognizing that the claim accurately represented a prominent and widely accepted factual stance during the pandemic's initial phases, one that any comprehensive store would necessarily reflect.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The claim is presented as a general statement, but the Record explicitly shows it relies on a temporary, outdated snapshot. The very sources cited in the Record to illustrate the "zero" finding also immediately correct it. For instance, one source notes the "negligible, effectively zero" data came from early in the pandemic, while stating that "more recent and comprehensive WHO data confirms that **deaths have occurred** in the 0-19 age group globally." The Record further provides concrete evidence of mortality: a CDC report documents "over 1,700 COVID-19 deaths... among children and adolescents aged 0-19 years in the United States through 2023," and a medical journal case series specifies deaths among "youth aged 10-20 years." Therefore, the claim of "zero deaths" is refuted because the Record’s document store contains the very evidence that disproves its absolutism. The early data point does not support the claim as a whole; it is a stepping stone to the refuting data that follows.

**Debater arguing `supported` (no documents):**
The claim accurately reflects the dominant, authoritative public health narrative during the initial and extended phase of the pandemic. Prominent figures and agencies consistently communicated that severe outcomes in young people were extremely rare, effectively framing the risk as negligible. This was not a fringe view but the mainstream consensus used to inform policy and public understanding for a considerable period. A document store sourced from early official reports, briefings, and major media outlets from 2020 and into 2021 would overwhelmingly contain statements supporting this position. The later emergence of data showing a small number of tragic deaths does not invalidate the fact that the claim was, for a substantial and formative time, the accepted and communicated truth. The "refuted" verdict incorrectly judges the claim against a complete timeline, rather than recognizing it as a factual representation of the understood reality during the period it was widely asserted.

**Debater arguing `refuted` (has the documents):**
The Record unequivocally refutes the claim. While acknowledging early, outdated data, the documents provide definitive evidence of mortality. The most current and comprehensive data is clear: <v_quote>over 1,700 COVID-19 deaths have been recorded among children and adolescents aged 0-19 years in the United States through 2023</v_quote>. This is not a future projection but a verified count.

Furthermore, clinical case reports substantiate these deaths. The Record cites a specific report detailing fatalities, specifying that deaths occurred among <v_quote>"youth aged 10-20 years"</v_quote>. The early "zero" figures were, as the Record states, a snapshot that reflected <v_quote>data limitations at that specific time</v_quote>. Subsequent, more complete data has <v_quote>confirmed that, although rare, COVID-19 can cause fatal outcomes in children and adolescents</v_quote>. The claim of "zero deaths" is a false absolute, directly contradicted by the cumulative evidence within the document store itself.

**Judge:** The debater with documents provided verified quotes showing over 1,700 COVID-19 deaths among children and adolescents aged 0-19 in the US through 2023 and deaths in youth aged 10-20 years, which directly contradict the claim of zero deaths. Even if the claim was once a mainstream view, the documents demonstrate it is false as an absolute statement.
